<p><b>Sir John Grey Gorton</b> <span style="font-size:85%;"><span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_Grand_Cross_of_the_Order_of_St_Michael_and_St_George" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight Grand Cross of the Order of St Michael and St George">GCMG</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of Australia">AC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_the_Companions_of_Honour" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of the Companions of Honour">CH</a></span></span> (9 September 1911 – 19 May 2002) was an Australian politician who served as the 19th <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Prime Minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a>, from 1968 to 1971, holding office as the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party of Australia</a>. Previously a <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">senator for Victoria</a>, he was the first and only member of the <a href="/wiki/Upper_house" title="Upper house">upper house</a> to assume the office of prime minister.
</p><p>Gorton was born out of wedlock and had a turbulent childhood. He studied at <a href="/wiki/Brasenose_College,_Oxford" title="Brasenose College, Oxford">Brasenose College, Oxford</a>, after finishing his secondary education at <a href="/wiki/Geelong_Grammar_School" title="Geelong Grammar School">Geelong Grammar School</a>, and then returned to Australia to take over his father's property in northern <a href="/wiki/Victoria_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Victoria (Australia)">Victoria</a>. Gorton enlisted in the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Australian_Air_Force" title="Royal Australian Air Force">Royal Australian Air Force</a> in 1940, and was a fighter pilot in <a href="/wiki/Malayan_Campaign" class="mw-redirect" title="Malayan Campaign">Malaya</a> and <a href="/wiki/New_Guinea_Campaign" class="mw-redirect" title="New Guinea Campaign">New Guinea</a> during the Second World War. He suffered severe facial injuries in a crash landing on <a href="/wiki/Bintan_Island" title="Bintan Island">Bintan Island</a> in 1942, and whilst being evacuated, his ship was torpedoed and sunk by a Japanese submarine. He returned to farming after being discharged in 1944, and was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Shire_of_Kerang" title="Shire of Kerang">Kerang Shire Council</a> in 1946; he later served a term as shire president. After a previous unsuccessful candidacy at state level, Gorton was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Senate</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1949_Australian_federal_election" title="1949 Australian federal election">1949 federal election</a>. He took a keen interest in foreign policy, and gained a reputation as a strident <a href="/wiki/Anti-Communist" class="mw-redirect" title="Anti-Communist">anti-Communist</a>. Gorton was promoted to the ministry in 1958, and over the following decade held a variety of different portfolios in the governments of <a href="/wiki/Robert_Menzies" title="Robert Menzies">Robert Menzies</a> and <a href="/wiki/Harold_Holt" title="Harold Holt">Harold Holt</a>. He was responsible at various times for the Navy, public works, education, and science. He was elevated to the Cabinet in 1966, and the following year, he was promoted to <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Government_in_the_Senate_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Government in the Senate (Australia)">Leader of the Government in the Senate</a>.
</p><p>Gorton <a href="/wiki/1968_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="1968 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">defeated</a> three other candidates for the Liberal leadership after <a href="/wiki/Disappearance_of_Harold_Holt" title="Disappearance of Harold Holt">Harold Holt's disappearance</a> on 17 December 1967. He became the first and only senator to assume the office of Prime Minister, but soon transferred to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a> in line with constitutional convention. The <a href="/wiki/Gorton_government" title="Gorton government">Gorton government</a> continued <a href="/wiki/Military_history_of_Australia_during_the_Vietnam_War" title="Military history of Australia during the Vietnam War">Australian involvement</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Vietnam_War" title="Vietnam War">Vietnam War</a>, but began withdrawing troops amid growing public discontent. It retained office at the <a href="/wiki/1969_Australian_federal_election" title="1969 Australian federal election">1969 federal election</a>, albeit with a severely reduced majority. After alienating much of his government, he resigned as Liberal leader in 1971 after a <a href="/wiki/Confidence_motion" class="mw-redirect" title="Confidence motion">confidence motion</a> in his leadership was tied, and was replaced by <a href="/wiki/William_McMahon" title="William McMahon">William McMahon</a>. After losing the premiership, Gorton was elected deputy leader under McMahon and appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Defence_(Australia)" title="Minister for Defence (Australia)">Minister for Defence</a>. He was sacked for disloyalty after a few months. After the Coalition's defeat at the <a href="/wiki/1972_Australian_federal_election" title="1972 Australian federal election">1972 federal election</a>, Gorton <a href="/wiki/1972_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="1972 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">unsuccessfully stood</a> as McMahon's replacement. He briefly was an opposition <a href="/wiki/Frontbencher" title="Frontbencher">frontbencher</a> under <a href="/wiki/Billy_Snedden" title="Billy Snedden">Billy Snedden</a>, but stood down in 1974 and spent the rest of his career as a <a href="/wiki/Backbencher" title="Backbencher">backbencher</a>. Gorton resigned from the Liberal Party when <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a> was elected leader and he denounced the <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_constitutional_crisis" title="1975 Australian constitutional crisis">dismissal</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Whitlam_government" title="Whitlam government">Whitlam government</a>; at the <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_federal_election" title="1975 Australian federal election">1975 election</a> he mounted an unsuccessful campaign for the Senate as an <a href="/wiki/Independent_politician" title="Independent politician">independent</a> in the ACT and advocated for a Labor win. He later spent several years as a political commentator, retiring from public life in 1981.
</p><p>Gorton's domestic policies, which emphasised <a href="/wiki/Centralisation" title="Centralisation">centralisation</a> and <a href="/wiki/Economic_nationalism" title="Economic nationalism">economic nationalism</a>, were often controversial in his own party, and his individualistic style alienated many of his Cabinet members. His political views widely varied and were incongruous, although he is generally regarded as having shifted further to the left over time after starting his parliamentary career on his party's hard right. Conservatively he opposed <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_land_rights_in_Australia" title="Indigenous land rights in Australia">Indigenous land rights</a>, was opposed to an Australian Republic, and at times fervently supported Australia <a href="/wiki/List_of_states_with_nuclear_weapons" title="List of states with nuclear weapons">developing nuclear weapons</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200269_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200269-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> but progressively, he staunchly supported drug decriminalisation, <a href="/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Australia" title="LGBT rights in Australia">LGBT equality</a> and <a href="/wiki/Reproductive_rights" title="Reproductive rights">reproductive rights</a>, having introduced the legislation nominally decriminalising homosexuality in Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Evaluations of his prime ministership have been mixed; although he is generally ranked higher than either Holt or McMahon, Gorton is usually considered to have been a transitional prime minister who ultimately fell short of his potential for greatness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002397-403_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002397-403-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_family_background">Birth and family background</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Birth and family background">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JohnGorton1913.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/JohnGorton1913.jpg/170px-JohnGorton1913.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="225" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/JohnGorton1913.jpg/255px-JohnGorton1913.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/JohnGorton1913.jpg/340px-JohnGorton1913.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1326" /></a><figcaption>Gorton as a toddler in 1913</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:NlaGortonmotherAlice1915.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/NlaGortonmotherAlice1915.jpg/170px-NlaGortonmotherAlice1915.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="212" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/NlaGortonmotherAlice1915.jpg/255px-NlaGortonmotherAlice1915.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/NlaGortonmotherAlice1915.jpg/340px-NlaGortonmotherAlice1915.jpg 2x" data-file-width="442" data-file-height="551" /></a><figcaption>Gorton as a child and his mother Alice in 1915</figcaption></figure>
<p>John Grey Gorton was the second child of Alice Sinn and John Rose Gorton; his older sister Ruth was born in 1909. He had no birth certificate, but official forms recorded his date of birth as 9 September 1911 and his place of birth as <a href="/wiki/Wellington" title="Wellington">Wellington</a>, New Zealand. His birth was registered in the state of <a href="/wiki/Victoria_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Victoria (Australia)">Victoria</a> as occurring on that date, but in the inner <a href="/wiki/Melbourne" title="Melbourne">Melbourne</a> suburb of <a href="/wiki/Prahran,_Victoria" class="mw-redirect" title="Prahran, Victoria">Prahran</a>. However, that document contained a number of inaccuracies – his name was given as "John Alga Gordon", his parents were recorded as husband and wife, his father's name was incorrect, and his sister was recorded as deceased.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> At some point before 1932, Gorton's father told him that he had actually been born in Wellington.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> There are no records of his birth in New Zealand, but his parents are known to have travelled there on several occasions.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> John Gorton apparently believed he was born in Wellington, listing the city as his place of birth on his RAAF enlistment papers, and claiming so to a biographer in 1968.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> If he were so, it would make him the only Australian Prime Minister born in New Zealand (and the second to have been originally a New Zealander, after <a href="/wiki/Chris_Watson" title="Chris Watson">Chris Watson</a>).
</p><p>If John Gorton was indeed born in New Zealand, this would have made him a New Zealand citizen from 1 January 1949 under changes to <a href="/wiki/New_Zealand_nationality_law" title="New Zealand nationality law">New Zealand nationality law</a>. Holding dual-citizenship would have rendered Gorton ineligible to sit in Australia's federal parliament under <a href="/wiki/Section_44_of_the_Australian_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="Section 44 of the Australian Constitution">Section 44 of the Australian Constitution</a>. Gorton's eligibility to have sat in parliament throughout his career is therefore unclear.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gorton's father was born to a middle-class family in <a href="/wiki/Manchester" title="Manchester">Manchester</a>, <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a>, UK. As a young man he moved to <a href="/wiki/Johannesburg" title="Johannesburg">Johannesburg</a>, South Africa, where he went into business as a merchant – during the <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Boer War</a>, he developed a reputation as a <a href="/wiki/War_profiteering" title="War profiteering">war profiteer</a>. He reputedly escaped the <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Ladysmith" title="Siege of Ladysmith">Siege of Ladysmith</a> by sneaking through Boer lines, and then made his way to Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-p3_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p3-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> He was involved in various business schemes in multiple states, and was to said to have "lived on the brink of a fortune which never quite materialised".<sup id="cite_ref-p4_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p4-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> One of his business partners was the inventor <a href="/wiki/George_Julius" title="George Julius">George Julius</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-p3_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p3-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> At some point, Gorton's father separated from his first wife, Kathleen O'Brien, and began living with Alice Sinn – born in Melbourne to a German father and an Irish mother.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> However, Kathleen refused to grant him a divorce. Some official documents record Gorton's parents as having married in New Zealand at some point, but there are no records of this occurring; any such marriage would have been bigamous. Gorton never denied his illegitimacy as an adult, but it did not become generally known until a biography was published during his premiership.<sup id="cite_ref-p4_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p4-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Childhood">Childhood</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Childhood">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Gorton_(seated_left),_teacher_and_other_senior_students,_Geelong_Grammar,_ca_1930.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/John_Gorton_%28seated_left%29%2C_teacher_and_other_senior_students%2C_Geelong_Grammar%2C_ca_1930.jpg/220px-John_Gorton_%28seated_left%29%2C_teacher_and_other_senior_students%2C_Geelong_Grammar%2C_ca_1930.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/John_Gorton_%28seated_left%29%2C_teacher_and_other_senior_students%2C_Geelong_Grammar%2C_ca_1930.jpg/330px-John_Gorton_%28seated_left%29%2C_teacher_and_other_senior_students%2C_Geelong_Grammar%2C_ca_1930.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/John_Gorton_%28seated_left%29%2C_teacher_and_other_senior_students%2C_Geelong_Grammar%2C_ca_1930.jpg/440px-John_Gorton_%28seated_left%29%2C_teacher_and_other_senior_students%2C_Geelong_Grammar%2C_ca_1930.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1056" data-file-height="706" /></a><figcaption>Gorton (seated, left) with other senior students and a teacher at Geelong Grammar School, about 1930</figcaption></figure>
<p>Gorton spent his early years living with his maternal grandparents in <a href="/wiki/Port_Melbourne,_Victoria" class="mw-redirect" title="Port Melbourne, Victoria">Port Melbourne</a>, as his parents were frequently away on business trips. When he was about four years old, his parents took him to live with them in Sydney, where they had an apartment at <a href="/wiki/Edgecliff,_New_South_Wales" title="Edgecliff, New South Wales">Edgecliff</a>. Gorton began his education at Edgecliff Preparatory School.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> When he was eight years old, his mother contracted tuberculosis and was sent to a <a href="/wiki/Sanatorium" title="Sanatorium">sanatorium</a> to avoid passing on the disease. She died in September 1920, aged 32. Gorton's grieving father sent his son to live with his estranged wife Kathleen. There, he met his sister Ruth for the first time; he had previously been told that she was dead. Although she was his full sibling, she had been raised by Kathleen since birth, and rarely saw her biological parents. Gorton initially lived with Kathleen and Ruth at their home in <a href="/wiki/Cronulla,_New_South_Wales" title="Cronulla, New South Wales">Cronulla</a>. They later moved into a larger house in <a href="/wiki/Killara,_New_South_Wales" class="mw-redirect" title="Killara, New South Wales">Killara</a>, in the north of Sydney.<sup id="cite_ref-hp8_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hp8-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While living in Killara, Gorton began attending Headfort College, a short-lived private school run by a former Anglican minister.<sup id="cite_ref-hp8_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hp8-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> In 1924, he began boarding at <a href="/wiki/Sydney_Church_of_England_Grammar_School" title="Sydney Church of England Grammar School">Sydney Church of England Grammar School</a> (Shore), initially on a weekly basis but later on a full-time one.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He did not excel academically, failing the <a href="/wiki/Intermediate_Certificate_(Australia)" title="Intermediate Certificate (Australia)">Intermediate Certificate</a> on his first attempt, but was a well-liked boy and good at sports.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Gorton began spending his holidays with his father, who had purchased a property in <a href="/wiki/Mystic_Park,_Victoria" class="mw-redirect" title="Mystic Park, Victoria">Mystic Park, Victoria</a>, and planted a citrus orchard.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> He left Shore at the end of 1926, and the following year began boarding at <a href="/wiki/Geelong_Grammar_School" title="Geelong Grammar School">Geelong Grammar School</a>, which he would attend for four years from 1927 to 1930.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He represented the school in athletics, <a href="/wiki/Australian_rules_football" title="Australian rules football">football</a>, and rowing, and in his final year was a school prefect and house captain.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="University">University</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: University">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After leaving Geelong Grammar, Gorton spent a year working on his father's property in Mystic Park. His father then took out a second mortgage to allow him to travel to England and attend <a href="/wiki/Oxford_University" class="mw-redirect" title="Oxford University">Oxford University</a>. Gorton arrived in England in early 1932, and after a period at a "cramming school" passed the exam to enter <a href="/wiki/Brasenose_College,_Oxford" title="Brasenose College, Oxford">Brasenose College</a>. He also took flying lessons around the same time, and was awarded a pilot's licence in June 1932. Gorton began his degree in October 1932 and finished in June 1935 with an "<a href="/wiki/Upper-second_class" class="mw-redirect" title="Upper-second class">upper second</a>" in history, politics and economics. He was initially something of an outsider, with relatively little money and no social connections. However, his prowess in rowing – he won the O.U.B.C. Fours in his first year, and was Captain of the <a href="/wiki/Brasenose_College_Boat_Club" title="Brasenose College Boat Club">Brasenose College Boat Club</a> – saw him elected to <a href="/wiki/Vincent%27s_Club" title="Vincent&#39;s Club">Vincent's Club</a> and <a href="/wiki/Leander_Club" title="Leander Club">Leander Club</a>. In 1934, while on holiday in <a href="/wiki/Second_Spanish_Republic" title="Second Spanish Republic">Spain</a>, he met his future wife <a href="/wiki/Bettina_Gorton" title="Bettina Gorton">Bettina Brown</a>, the younger sister of one of his College friends. They married in early 1935.<sup id="cite_ref-bdas_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bdas-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Return_to_Australia">Return to Australia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Return to Australia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After his graduation, Gorton and his wife returned to Australia via the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a>, spending some time with her family in <a href="/wiki/Maine" title="Maine">Maine</a>. He had expected to take up a position at <a href="/wiki/The_Herald_and_Weekly_Times" title="The Herald and Weekly Times">The Herald and Weekly Times</a>, <a href="/wiki/Keith_Murdoch" title="Keith Murdoch">Keith Murdoch</a>'s newspaper group. However, he arrived in Melbourne to find his father in failing health; he died in August 1936. Gorton had taken over the management of his father's orchard as soon as he entered hospital. He inherited an overdraft of £5,000, which took several years to pay off. However, the property – located on the western side of <a href="/wiki/North_Victorian_Wetlands" title="North Victorian Wetlands">Kangaroo Lake</a> – was in good condition, requiring only minor improvements. He employed up to ten seasonal workers during picking season. Mystic Park remained his primary residence until he was appointed to the ministry, at which point he and his family moved to Canberra.<sup id="cite_ref-bdas_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bdas-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_service">Military service</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Military service">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="1940.E2.80.931942"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1940–1942">1940–1942</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: 1940–1942">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JohnGortonWW21941.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/JohnGortonWW21941.jpg/170px-JohnGortonWW21941.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="214" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/JohnGortonWW21941.jpg/255px-JohnGortonWW21941.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/JohnGortonWW21941.jpg/340px-JohnGortonWW21941.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1258" /></a><figcaption>Pilot Officer Gorton</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JohnGorton1941.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/JohnGorton1941.jpg/170px-JohnGorton1941.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="233" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/JohnGorton1941.jpg/255px-JohnGorton1941.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/JohnGorton1941.jpg/340px-JohnGorton1941.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1373" /></a><figcaption>Gorton prior to leaving for war service in 1941</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 31 May 1940, following the outbreak of World War II, Gorton enlisted in the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Australian_Air_Force" title="Royal Australian Air Force">Royal Australian Air Force</a> Reserve.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> At the age of 29, Gorton was considered too old for pilot training, but he re-applied in September after this rule was relaxed. Gorton was accepted and commissioned into the RAAF on 8 November 1940.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He trained as a fighter pilot at <a href="/wiki/Somers,_Victoria" title="Somers, Victoria">Somers, Victoria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Wagga_Wagga" title="Wagga Wagga">Wagga Wagga</a>, <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales" title="New South Wales">New South Wales</a>, before being sent to the UK. Gorton completed his training at <a href="/wiki/RAF_Heston" class="mw-redirect" title="RAF Heston">RAF Heston</a> and <a href="/wiki/RAF_Honiley" title="RAF Honiley">RAF Honiley</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Trengrove:_71_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Trengrove:_71-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> with <a href="/w/index.php?title=No._61_Operational_Training_Unit_RAF&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="No. 61 Operational Training Unit RAF (page does not exist)">No. 61 Operational Training Unit RAF</a>, flying <a href="/wiki/Supermarine_Spitfire" title="Supermarine Spitfire">Supermarine Spitfires</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Hancock:_33_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hancock:_33-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> He was disappointed when his first operational posting was <a href="/wiki/No._135_Squadron_RAF" title="No. 135 Squadron RAF">No. 135 Squadron RAF</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Hawker_Hurricane" title="Hawker Hurricane">Hawker Hurricane</a> unit, as he considered the type greatly inferior to Spitfires.<sup id="cite_ref-Trengrove:_71_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Trengrove:_71-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During late 1941, Gorton and other members of his squadron became part of the <a href="/wiki/En_cadre" class="mw-redirect" title="En cadre">cadre</a> of a Hurricane <a href="/wiki/Wing_(air_force_unit)" class="mw-redirect" title="Wing (air force unit)">wing</a> being formed for service in the Middle East. They were sent by sea, with 50 Hurricanes in crates, travelling around Africa to reduce the risk of attack. In December, when the ship was at <a href="/wiki/Durban" title="Durban">Durban</a>, South Africa, it was diverted to Singapore, after <a href="/wiki/Pacific_War" title="Pacific War">Japan entered the war</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> As it approached its destination in mid-January, <a href="/wiki/Empire_of_Japan" title="Empire of Japan">Japanese</a> forces were <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Malaya" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Malaya">advancing down the Malayan Peninsula</a>. The ship was attacked on at least one occasion by Japanese aircraft, but arrived and unloaded safely after tropical storms made enemy air raids impossible.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> As the Hurricanes were assembled, the pilots were formed into a composite operational squadron, <a href="/wiki/No._232_Squadron_RAF" title="No. 232 Squadron RAF">No. 232 Squadron RAF</a>.
</p><p>In late January 1942, the squadron became operational and joined the remnants of several others that had been in Malaya, operating out of <a href="/wiki/Seletar_Airport" title="Seletar Airport">RAF Seletar</a> and RAF <a href="/wiki/Kallang" title="Kallang">Kallang</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> During one of his first <a href="/wiki/Sortie" title="Sortie">sorties</a>, Gorton was involved in a brief dogfight over the <a href="/wiki/South_China_Sea" title="South China Sea">South China Sea</a>, after which he suffered engine failure and was forced to land on <a href="/wiki/Bintan" class="mw-redirect" title="Bintan">Bintan</a> island,<sup id="cite_ref-Hancock:_33_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hancock:_33-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> 40&#160;km (25&#160;mi) south east of Singapore. As he landed, one of the Hurricane's wheels hit an embankment and flipped over. Gorton was not properly strapped in and his face hit the gun sight and windscreen, mutilating his nose and breaking both cheekbones.<sup id="cite_ref-Trengrove:_76_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Trengrove:_76-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> He also suffered severe lacerations to both arms. He made his way out of the wreck and was rescued by members of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Dutch_East_Indies_Army" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal Dutch East Indies Army">Royal Dutch East Indies Army</a>, who provided some medical treatment. Gorton later claimed that his face was so badly cut and bruised, that a member of the RAF sent to collect him assumed he was near death, collected his personal effects and returned to Singapore without him.<sup id="cite_ref-Trengrove:_76_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Trengrove:_76-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> By chance, one week later, Sgt Matt O'Mara of <a href="/wiki/No._453_Squadron_RAAF" title="No. 453 Squadron RAAF">No. 453 Squadron RAAF</a> also crash landed on Bintan, and arranged for them to be collected.<sup id="cite_ref-Trengrove:_77_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Trengrove:_77-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>They arrived back in Singapore, on 11 February, three days after <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Singapore" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Singapore">the island had been invaded</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Trengrove:_77_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Trengrove:_77-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> As the Allied air force units on Singapore had been destroyed or evacuated by this stage, Gorton was put on the <i>Derrymore</i>, an ammunition ship bound for <a href="/wiki/Jakarta" title="Jakarta">Batavia</a> (Jakarta). On 13 February, as it neared its destination, the ship was torpedoed by Japanese submarine I-55 <a href="/wiki/Kaidai_class_submarine" class="mw-redirect" title="Kaidai class submarine">Kaidai class submarine</a> and the <i>Derrymore</i> was abandoned. Gorton then spent almost a day on a crowded liferaft, in shark-infested waters, with little drinking water, until the raft was spotted by <a href="/wiki/HMAS_Ballarat_(J184)" title="HMAS Ballarat (J184)">HMAS <i>Ballarat</i></a>, which picked up the passengers and took them to Batavia.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:77_Sqn_RAAF_pilots_%26_Kittyhawk_(AWM_NWA0019).JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/77_Sqn_RAAF_pilots_%26_Kittyhawk_%28AWM_NWA0019%29.JPG/220px-77_Sqn_RAAF_pilots_%26_Kittyhawk_%28AWM_NWA0019%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="169" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/77_Sqn_RAAF_pilots_%26_Kittyhawk_%28AWM_NWA0019%29.JPG/330px-77_Sqn_RAAF_pilots_%26_Kittyhawk_%28AWM_NWA0019%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/77_Sqn_RAAF_pilots_%26_Kittyhawk_%28AWM_NWA0019%29.JPG/440px-77_Sqn_RAAF_pilots_%26_Kittyhawk_%28AWM_NWA0019%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="624" data-file-height="478" /></a><figcaption>Pilots of B Flight, No. 77 Squadron with a P-40 Kittyhawk in the Northern Territory, January 1943. Gorton is fourth left in the back row.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Two schoolfriends, who had also been evacuated from Singapore to Batavia, heard that Gorton was in hospital, arranged for them to be put on a ship for <a href="/wiki/Fremantle" title="Fremantle">Fremantle</a>, which left on 23 February and treated Gorton's wounds.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> When the ship arrived in Fremantle, on 3 March, one of Gorton's arm wounds had become septic and needed extensive treatment. However, he was more concerned about the effect that the sight of his mutilated face would have on his wife. It is reported that Betty Gorton, who had been running the farm in his absence, was relieved to see Gorton alive.<sup id="cite_ref-Hancock:_33_22-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hancock:_33-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="1942.E2.80.931944"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1942–1944">1942–1944</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: 1942–1944">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After arriving in Australia he was posted to <a href="/wiki/Darwin,_Northern_Territory" title="Darwin, Northern Territory">Darwin</a> on 12 August 1942 with <a href="/wiki/No._77_Squadron_RAAF" title="No. 77 Squadron RAAF">No. 77 Squadron RAAF</a> (Kittyhawks). During this time he was involved in his second air accident.  While flying <a href="/wiki/P-40E" class="mw-redirect" title="P-40E">P-40E</a> A29-60 on 7 September 1942, he was forced to land due to an incorrectly set fuel cock. Both Gorton and his aircraft were recovered several days later after spending time in the bush. On 21 February 1943 the squadron was relocated to <a href="/wiki/Milne_Bay" title="Milne Bay">Milne Bay</a>, <a href="/wiki/New_Guinea" title="New Guinea">New Guinea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-naaws_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naaws-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gorton's final air incident came on 18 March 1943. His A29-192 Kittyhawk's engine failed on takeoff, causing the aircraft to flip at the end of the strip. Gorton was unhurt. In March 1944, Gorton was sent back to Australia with the rank of Flight Lieutenant. His final posting was as a Flying Instructor with <a href="/wiki/No._2_Operational_Conversion_Unit_RAAF" title="No. 2 Operational Conversion Unit RAAF">No. 2 Operational Training Unit</a> at <a href="/wiki/Mildura" title="Mildura">Mildura</a>, Victoria.  He was then discharged from the RAAF on 5 December 1944.<sup id="cite_ref-naaws_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naaws-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During late 1944 Gorton went to <a href="/wiki/Austin_Hospital,_Melbourne" class="mw-redirect" title="Austin Hospital, Melbourne">Heidelberg Hospital</a> for surgery which could not fully repair his facial injuries; he was left permanently disfigured.<sup id="cite_ref-naaws_31-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naaws-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_involvement">Early political involvement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Early political involvement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>After resuming his life in Mystic Park, Gorton was elected unopposed to the <a href="/wiki/Shire_of_Kerang" title="Shire of Kerang">Kerang Shire Council</a> in September 1946. He remained on the council until 1952, overlapping with his first term in the Senate, and from 1949 to 1950 was shire president.<sup id="cite_ref-p46_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p46-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Although he had little previous experience, Gorton began to develop a reputation as a powerful public speaker. His first major speech, in April 1946, was an address to a welcome-home gathering for returned soldiers at the Mystic Park Hall. In what <a href="/wiki/John_Brogden_(politician)" title="John Brogden (politician)">John Brogden</a> has described as "Australia's best unknown political speech",<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> he exhorted his audience to honour those who had died in the war by building "a world in which meanness and poverty, tyranny and hate, have no existence". Gorton's next major speech was made in September 1947, at a rally against the <a href="/wiki/Chifley_government" title="Chifley government">Chifley government</a>'s attempt to nationalise private banks. He told the crowd in <a href="/wiki/Kerang" title="Kerang">Kerang</a> that they should oppose the establishment of banks run by politicians, and objected in particular to the government's decision not to take the issue to a referendum.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> According to his biographer Ian Hancock, "the bank nationalisation issue marked his advance beyond purely local politics and stamped him firmly and publicly as an anti-socialist".<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gorton had been a supporter of the <a href="/wiki/Country_Party_(Victoria)" class="mw-redirect" title="Country Party (Victoria)">Country Party</a> before the war, along with most of his neighbours. Over time, he became frustrated with the party's frequent squabbles with the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_(Victorian_Division)" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia (Victorian Division)">Liberal Party</a> and its willingness to cooperate with the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_(Victorian_Branch)" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party (Victorian Branch)">Labor Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> After the Victorian Country Party withdrew from <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">its coalition</a> with the Liberals in December 1948, Gorton became involved in efforts to form a new anti-socialist movement that would absorb both parties. At some point he was introduced to <a href="/wiki/Magnus_Cormack" title="Magnus Cormack">Magnus Cormack</a>, the state president of the Liberals, who became something of a mentor.<sup id="cite_ref-p53_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p53-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In March 1949, Gorton was elected to the state executive of the new organisation, which named itself the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_and_Country_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal and Country Party">Liberal and Country Party</a> (LCP).<sup id="cite_ref-p55_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p55-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> On a number of occasions he addressed Country Party gatherings, urging its members to join the new party and stressing that it would not neglect rural interests, as many feared.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> However, the LCP did not achieve its goal of uniting the anti-Labor forces, as most Country Party members viewed it as simply a takeover attempt; the new party affiliated with the federal <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party of Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-p53_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p53-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 1949, Gorton stood for the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_Legislative_Council" title="Victorian Legislative Council">Victorian Legislative Council</a> as the LCP candidate in <a href="/wiki/Northern_Province_(Victoria)" title="Northern Province (Victoria)">Northern Province</a>. It was a safe Country Party seat, and at the preceding three elections no other parties had bothered to field a candidate.<sup id="cite_ref-p55_38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p55-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Making right-wing unity the focal point of his campaign, Gorton polled 48.8 percent of the vote to finish less than 400 votes behind the sitting member, <a href="/wiki/George_Tuckett_(politician)" title="George Tuckett (politician)">George Tuckett</a>. The result impressed the LCP's leadership,<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> and the following month he was <a href="/wiki/Preselection" title="Preselection">preselected</a> in third place on its joint <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Senate</a> ticket with the Country Party. He was relatively unknown within the party, and his rural background was a major factor in his selection.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> The Coalition won a large majority at the <a href="/wiki/1949_Australian_federal_election" title="1949 Australian federal election">1949 federal election</a>, including four out of Victoria's seven senators. The LCP candidates joined the parliamentary Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Senate_.281950.E2.80.931968.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Senate_(1950–1968)">Senate (1950–1968)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Senate (1950–1968)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Backbencher">Backbencher</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Backbencher">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JohnGorton1954.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/bf/JohnGorton1954.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="248" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="170" data-file-height="248" /></a><figcaption>Gorton in 1954</figcaption></figure>
<p>Gorton's term in the Senate began on 22 February 1950.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200261_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200261-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> He was re-elected to additional terms at the <a href="/wiki/1951_Australian_federal_election" title="1951 Australian federal election">1951</a>, <a href="/wiki/1953_Australian_Senate_election" title="1953 Australian Senate election">1953</a>, <a href="/wiki/1958_Australian_federal_election" title="1958 Australian federal election">1958</a>, and <a href="/wiki/1964_Australian_Senate_election" title="1964 Australian Senate election">1964 elections</a>, and from 1953 occupied first place on the Coalition's ticket in Victoria. Gorton's early speeches on domestic policy foreshadowed the stances and policy initiatives he would later adopt as prime minister, such as economic nationalism, support for a strong central government, and support for nuclear energy. One of his first notable actions in the Senate came in November 1951, when he successfully moved a motion opposing "any substantial measure of ownership or control over any Australian broadcasting station" being granted to non-Australians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200273_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200273-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In his early speeches on foreign policy, Gorton drew analogies between the actions of the Soviet Union and Communist China and those of Nazi Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200268_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200268-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> He developed a reputation as a "hardline <a href="/wiki/Anti-communism" title="Anti-communism">anti-communist</a>", speaking in favour of the Communist Party Dissolution Bill and campaigning for the "Yes" vote during the <a href="/wiki/1951_Australian_Communist_Party_ban_referendum" title="1951 Australian Communist Party ban referendum">1951 referendum</a> to ban the <a href="/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Australia" title="Communist Party of Australia">Communist Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200264–66_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200264–66-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> At a campaign rally in September 1951, he had to be restrained by police after attempting to drag a heckler from his seat and shouting at him to "come outside, you yellow rat".<sup id="cite_ref-:0_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From 1952 to 1958, Gorton served on the Joint Parliamentary Committee on Foreign Affairs, including as chairman for a period. He developed a keen interest in Asia, which was rooted in his anti-communism, and joined parliamentary delegations to Malaya, South Vietnam, <a href="/wiki/Thailand" title="Thailand">Thailand</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Philippines" title="Philippines">Philippines</a>. He strongly supported Australia joining the <a href="/wiki/Southeast_Asia_Treaty_Organisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Southeast Asia Treaty Organisation">Southeast Asia Treaty Organisation</a> (SEATO), a collective defence initiative designed to <a href="/wiki/Domino_theory" title="Domino theory">prevent the spread of communism</a> in the region. He also supported <a href="/wiki/Taiwanese_independence" class="mw-redirect" title="Taiwanese independence">Taiwanese independence</a> and opposed Australian recognition of the People's Republic of China (PRC).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200267_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200267-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> On a number of occasions, Gorton spoke of the need for Australia to develop a foreign policy independent of Britain and the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200268_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200268-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> In May 1957, he told the Senate that Australia should acquire its own nuclear deterrent, including intercontinental missiles.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200269_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200269-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Government_minister">Government minister</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Government minister">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Navy">Navy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Navy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Paul_Hasluck,_Shen_Chang-huan,_John_Gorton.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Paul_Hasluck%2C_Shen_Chang-huan%2C_John_Gorton.jpg/220px-Paul_Hasluck%2C_Shen_Chang-huan%2C_John_Gorton.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="174" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Paul_Hasluck%2C_Shen_Chang-huan%2C_John_Gorton.jpg/330px-Paul_Hasluck%2C_Shen_Chang-huan%2C_John_Gorton.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/18/Paul_Hasluck%2C_Shen_Chang-huan%2C_John_Gorton.jpg 2x" data-file-width="425" data-file-height="337" /></a><figcaption>Gorton in 1965 with External Affairs Minister <a href="/wiki/Paul_Hasluck" title="Paul Hasluck">Paul Hasluck</a> and Taiwanese foreign minister <a href="/wiki/Shen_Chang-huan" title="Shen Chang-huan">Shen Chang-huan</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Gorton was elevated to the ministry after the <a href="/wiki/1958_Australian_federal_election" title="1958 Australian federal election">1958 election</a>, as <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_the_Navy_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for the Navy (Australia)">Minister for the Navy</a>. This was a junior position subordinate to the Minister for Defence, <a href="/wiki/Athol_Townley" title="Athol Townley">Athol Townley</a>. Although his promotion was unexpected,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200281_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200281-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> he would serve as Minister for the Navy for more than five years, the longest-serving navy minister in Australia's history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200296_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200296-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Gorton regularly attended meetings of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Commonwealth_Naval_Board" title="Australian Commonwealth Naval Board">Naval Board</a>, unlike previous ministers, and championed its recommendations at cabinet meetings. He was able to secure most elements of the board's desired modernisation program, despite Townley showing more interest in the air force. During Gorton's tenure, the navy acquired four Australian-built frigates and six British-built minesweepers, as well as placing orders for three <a href="/wiki/Charles_F._Adams-class_destroyer" title="Charles F. Adams-class destroyer"><i>Charles F. Adams</i>-class destroyers</a> and four <a href="/wiki/Oberon-class_submarine" title="Oberon-class submarine"><i>Oberon</i>-class submarines</a>. He postponed the phasing out of the <a href="/wiki/Fleet_Air_Arm_(RAN)" title="Fleet Air Arm (RAN)">Fleet Air Arm</a>, due to occur in 1963, and secured the purchase of 27 <a href="/wiki/Westland_Wessex" title="Westland Wessex">Westland Wessex</a> helicopters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200287–90_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200287–90-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gorton was a supporter and admirer of Robert Menzies, who was sympathetic to his ambitions for higher office and assigned him additional responsibilities. In 1959, he was tasked with securing the passage of the Matrimonial Causes Bill, which introduced uniform divorce laws; he described it as "a systematic attempt to make a new approach to the problem of divorce". The bill was opposed by religious conservatives (mostly Catholics) on both sides of politics, but it eventually passed the Senate with only a single amendment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200285–86_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200285–86-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Gorton was appointed assistant minister to the <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Foreign_Affairs_(Australia)" title="Minister for Foreign Affairs (Australia)">Minister for External Affairs</a> in February 1960, working under Menzies and later under <a href="/wiki/Garfield_Barwick" title="Garfield Barwick">Garfield Barwick</a> and <a href="/wiki/Paul_Hasluck" title="Paul Hasluck">Paul Hasluck</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200284_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200284-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> In February 1962, he was also made minister-in-charge of the <a href="/wiki/CSIRO" title="CSIRO">CSIRO</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200296_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200296-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> During <a href="/wiki/Question_time" title="Question time">question time</a> in the Senate, he represented ministers from the House of Representatives, allowing him to gain experience in portfolios beyond his own.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock200284_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock200284-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Education_and_science">Education and science</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Education and science">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Gorton,_Fred_White,_Ren%C3%A9_Maheu.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/John_Gorton%2C_Fred_White%2C_Ren%C3%A9_Maheu.jpg/220px-John_Gorton%2C_Fred_White%2C_Ren%C3%A9_Maheu.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="172" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/John_Gorton%2C_Fred_White%2C_Ren%C3%A9_Maheu.jpg/330px-John_Gorton%2C_Fred_White%2C_Ren%C3%A9_Maheu.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/47/John_Gorton%2C_Fred_White%2C_Ren%C3%A9_Maheu.jpg 2x" data-file-width="425" data-file-height="333" /></a><figcaption>Gorton with CSIRO chairman <a href="/wiki/Fred_White_(scientist)" class="mw-redirect" title="Fred White (scientist)">Fred White</a> and UNESCO Director-General <a href="/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Maheu" title="René Maheu">René Maheu</a> at a regional science organisation meeting in 1964</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the <a href="/wiki/1963_Australian_federal_election" title="1963 Australian federal election">1963 election</a>, Gorton relinquished the navy portfolio and was placed in charge of the government's activities in education and scientific research.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> He was also made <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Works_(1952%E2%80%9373)" class="mw-redirect" title="Department of Works (1952–73)">Minister for Works</a> and <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Interior_(Australia)" title="Minister for Interior (Australia)">Minister for the Interior</a>, relatively low-profile positions dealing mostly with administrative matters; the latter post was taken over by <a href="/wiki/Doug_Anthony" title="Doug Anthony">Doug Anthony</a> after a few months. Menzies had a personal interest in education, having previously handled the portfolio himself,<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> and told Gorton that it should be his primary focus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002103_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002103-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> He was given oversight of the Australian Universities Commission, the <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_University" title="Australian National University">Australian National University</a>, the <a href="/wiki/National_Archives_of_Australia" title="National Archives of Australia">Commonwealth Archives Office</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Literary_Fund" title="Commonwealth Literary Fund">Commonwealth Literary Fund</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/National_Library_of_Australia" title="National Library of Australia">National Library of Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002104_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002104-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> In January 1966, Menzies retired and <a href="/wiki/Harold_Holt" title="Harold Holt">Harold Holt</a> became prime minister. Gorton was elevated to <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Australia" title="Cabinet of Australia">cabinet</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002100_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002100-59">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> and at the end of the year was given the title <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Education_(Australia)" title="Minister for Education (Australia)">Minister for Education and Science</a>. He was placed in charge of the new <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Education_and_Science_(Australia)" title="Department of Education and Science (Australia)">Department of Education and Science</a>, the first time those portfolios had been given a separate department at federal level.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JohnGorton.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/JohnGorton.jpg/150px-JohnGorton.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="198" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/JohnGorton.jpg/225px-JohnGorton.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/JohnGorton.jpg/300px-JohnGorton.jpg 2x" data-file-width="425" data-file-height="561" /></a><figcaption>Senator Gorton in 1967</figcaption></figure>
<p>Gorton presided over a "major intrusion" of the federal government into the education sector.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002121_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002121-61">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> His tenure saw significant increases in the number of university scholarships available, the number of new university entrants, and overall funding of education.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> One of the first major issues Gorton confronted was that of state aid to non-government schools. He believed that private schools should have equal access to federal government funding, and in 1964 announced that the government would fund science laboratories for private schools.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002109–111_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002109–111-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> This stance, supported by the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_education_in_Australia" title="Catholic education in Australia">Catholic education sector</a> but opposed by secularists and most Protestant schools, eventually achieved widespread acceptance.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> In September 1965, Gorton created the Commonwealth Advisory Committee on Advanced Education, tasked with advising the government on non-university <a href="/wiki/Technical_and_further_education" title="Technical and further education">technical and further education</a> (TAFE). He announced that the federal government would fund technical colleges on a pro-rata basis with the states,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002111–112_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002111–112-66">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> and personally oversaw the establishment of the Canberra College of Advanced Education, the forerunner of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Canberra" title="University of Canberra">University of Canberra</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002114–115_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002114–115-67">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> As science minister, he lent his support to the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Australian_Telescope" title="Anglo-Australian Telescope">Anglo-Australian Telescope</a> project, securing cabinet approval for its construction in April 1967.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002120–121_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002120–121-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Role_in_the_VIP_aircraft_affair">Role in the <a href="/wiki/VIP_aircraft_affair" title="VIP aircraft affair">VIP aircraft affair</a></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Role in the VIP aircraft affair">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Gorton's reputation was significantly enhanced by his role in the <a href="/wiki/VIP_aircraft_affair" title="VIP aircraft affair">VIP aircraft affair</a>, a political controversy relating to the use of <a href="/wiki/Royal_Australian_Air_Force" title="Royal Australian Air Force">Royal Australian Air Force</a> (RAAF) <a href="/wiki/Royal_Australian_Air_Force_VIP_aircraft" title="Royal Australian Air Force VIP aircraft">VIP aircraft</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Holt_government" title="Holt government">Holt government</a> and its predecessor the <a href="/wiki/Menzies_government_(1949%E2%80%9366)" class="mw-redirect" title="Menzies government (1949–66)">Menzies government</a> that came to a head in October 1967. Holt had provided vague and inaccurate answers to <a href="/wiki/Question_time" title="Question time">parliamentary questions</a> about the VIP fleet, notably denying the existence of <a href="/wiki/Manifest_(transportation)" title="Manifest (transportation)">passenger manifests</a> which might confirm instances of misuse.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock20031_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock20031-69">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Air Minister <a href="/wiki/Peter_Howson_(politician)" title="Peter Howson (politician)">Peter Howson</a> became aware of the inaccuracies and sought to protect Holt, but their statements were soon subjected to further scrutiny, leading to accusations that they had conspired to <a href="/wiki/Misleading_parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Misleading parliament">mislead parliament</a>. Gorton, who had just replaced <a href="/wiki/Denham_Henty" title="Denham Henty">Denham Henty</a> as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Government_in_the_Senate_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Government in the Senate (Australia)">Leader of the Government in the Senate</a> on 16 October, helped resolve the situation by <a href="/wiki/Table_(parliamentary_procedure)" title="Table (parliamentary procedure)">tabling</a> the "missing" passenger manifests in their entirety on 25 October. He did so on the grounds that the government could not maintain such a cover-up.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002128_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002128-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gorton's act has been credited for helping thrust him into general public view; boosting his standing among his parliamentary colleagues; and for the first time Gorton began to be viewed as a serious future leadership contender, which proved to be critical to his election as Holt's successor <a href="/wiki/1968_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="1968 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">three months later</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002131–132_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002131–132-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> However, although Gorton defended Howson in Parliament,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002129_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002129-72">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> the affair also sewed the seeds of long-term enmity between him and Howson, the latter turning into a staunch, vocal opponent of Gorton during his years as Prime Minister and beyond.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002131–132_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002131–132-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister">Prime Minister</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Prime Minister">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Gorton_government" title="Gorton government">Gorton government</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_term">First term</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: First term">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span id="Holt.27s_disappearance"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Holt's_disappearance">Holt's disappearance</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Holt&#039;s disappearance">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Disappearance_of_Harold_Holt" title="Disappearance of Harold Holt">Disappearance of Harold Holt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election,_1968" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia leadership election, 1968">Liberal Party of Australia leadership election, 1968</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Gorton_Swearing_In.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/John_Gorton_Swearing_In.jpg/220px-John_Gorton_Swearing_In.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="218" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/John_Gorton_Swearing_In.jpg/330px-John_Gorton_Swearing_In.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/9b/John_Gorton_Swearing_In.jpg 2x" data-file-width="425" data-file-height="421" /></a><figcaption>Gorton being sworn in as Prime Minister on 10 January 1968</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Harold_Holt" title="Harold Holt">Harold Holt</a> disappeared while swimming on 17 December 1967 and was declared presumed drowned two days later. His presumed successor was Liberal deputy leader <a href="/wiki/William_McMahon" title="William McMahon">William McMahon</a>.  However, on 18 December, the Country Party leader and Deputy Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/John_McEwen" title="John McEwen">John McEwen</a> announced that if McMahon were named the new Liberal leader, he and his party would not serve under him.  His reasons were never stated publicly, but in a private meeting with McMahon, he said "I will not serve under you because I do not trust you".<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> McEwen's shock declaration triggered a leadership crisis within the Liberal Party; even more significantly, it raised the threat of a possible breaking of the Coalition, which would spell electoral disaster for the Liberals.  Up to that time, the Liberals had never won enough seats in any House of Representatives election to be able to govern without Country Party support.  Indeed, since the Coalition's formation in 1923, the major non-<a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Labor</a> party had only been able to govern alone once, during <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Lyons" title="Joseph Lyons">Joseph Lyons</a>' first ministry. Even then, Lyons' <a href="/wiki/United_Australia_Party" title="United Australia Party">United Australia Party</a> had come up four seats short of a majority in its own right, and was only able to garner a bare majority of two when the five members of the <a href="/wiki/Emergency_Committee_of_South_Australia" title="Emergency Committee of South Australia">Emergency Committee of South Australia</a> joined the UAP party room.
</p><p>The Governor-General <a href="/wiki/Richard_Casey,_Baron_Casey" title="Richard Casey, Baron Casey">Lord Casey</a> swore McEwen in as Prime Minister, on an interim basis pending the Liberal Party electing its new leader. McEwen agreed to accept an interim appointment provided there was no formal statement of time limit. This appointment was in keeping with previous occasions when a conservative Coalition government had been deprived of its leader.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Casey also concurred in the view put to him by McEwen that to commission a Liberal temporarily as Prime Minister would give that person an unfair advantage in the forthcoming party room ballot for the permanent leader.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Gorton_1968_colour.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/John_Gorton_1968_colour.jpg/220px-John_Gorton_1968_colour.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="183" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/John_Gorton_1968_colour.jpg/330px-John_Gorton_1968_colour.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/John_Gorton_1968_colour.jpg/440px-John_Gorton_1968_colour.jpg 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="581" /></a><figcaption>Gorton in his office in 1968</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the subsequent <a href="/wiki/1968_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="1968 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">leadership struggle</a>, Gorton was championed by Army Minister <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a>, Government Whip in the Senate <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Scott_(politician)" title="Malcolm Scott (politician)">Malcolm Scott</a> and Liberal Party Whip <a href="/wiki/Dudley_Erwin" title="Dudley Erwin">Dudley Erwin</a>, and with their support he was able to defeat rivals <a href="/wiki/Paul_Hasluck" title="Paul Hasluck">Paul Hasluck</a>, <a href="/wiki/Les_Bury" title="Les Bury">Les Bury</a> and <a href="/wiki/Billy_Snedden" title="Billy Snedden">Billy Snedden</a> to become Liberal leader even though he was a member of the Senate.  He was elected party leader on 9 January 1968, and appointed Prime Minister on 10 January, replacing McEwen.  He was the only Senator in Australia's history to be Prime Minister and the only Prime Minister to have ever served in the Senate.  He remained a Senator until, in accordance with the <a href="/wiki/Westminster_system" title="Westminster system">Westminster</a> tradition that the Prime Minister is a member of the lower house of parliament, he resigned on 1 February 1968 to <a href="/wiki/1968_Higgins_by-election" title="1968 Higgins by-election">contest the by-election</a> for Holt's old House of Representatives seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Higgins" title="Division of Higgins">Higgins</a> in south Melbourne.  The by-election in this comfortably safe Liberal seat was held on 24 February; there were three other candidates, but Gorton achieved a massive 68% of the formal vote.  He visited all the polling places during the day, but was unable to vote for himself as he was still enrolled in <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Mallee" title="Division of Mallee">Mallee</a>, in rural western Victoria.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Between 2 and 23 February (both dates inclusive) he was a member of neither house of parliament.
</p><p>Although he was still a Senator when he was sworn in as Prime Minister, Gorton never sat in the Senate as Prime Minister because neither House of Parliament was in session between his swearing in as Prime Minister and his resignation from the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Activities_in_office">Activities in office</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Activities in office">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Gorton_1969.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/John_Gorton_1969.jpg/170px-John_Gorton_1969.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="172" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/John_Gorton_1969.jpg/255px-John_Gorton_1969.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/John_Gorton_1969.jpg/340px-John_Gorton_1969.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="911" /></a><figcaption>Gorton at the <a href="/wiki/Honeysuckle_Creek_Tracking_Station" title="Honeysuckle Creek Tracking Station">Honeysuckle Creek Tracking Station</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Apollo_11" title="Apollo 11">Apollo 11</a> moon landing in 1969</figcaption></figure>
<p>Gorton was initially a very popular Prime Minister. He carved out a style quite distinct from those of his predecessors – the aloof Menzies and the affable, sporty Holt. Gorton liked to portray himself as a man of the people who enjoyed a beer and a gamble, with a bit of a <a href="/wiki/Larrikinism" class="mw-redirect" title="Larrikinism">"larrikin"</a> streak about him – hence one of his nicknames, "Gort the sport". Unfortunately for him, this reputation later came back to haunt him.
</p><p>He also began to follow new policies, pursuing independent defence and foreign policies and distancing Australia from its traditional ties to Britain. But he continued to support Australia's involvement in the <a href="/wiki/Vietnam_War" title="Vietnam War">Vietnam War</a>, a position he had reluctantly inherited from Holt, which became increasingly unpopular after 1968. On domestic issues, he favoured centralist policies at the expense of the states, which alienated powerful Liberal state leaders like Sir <a href="/wiki/Henry_Bolte" title="Henry Bolte">Henry Bolte</a> of Victoria and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Askin" title="Robert Askin">Bob Askin</a> of New South Wales. He also fostered an independent <a href="/wiki/Australian_film_industry" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian film industry">Australian film industry</a> and increased government funding for the arts.
</p><p>Gorton proved to be a surprisingly poor media performer and public speaker, and was portrayed by the media as a foolish and incompetent administrator. He was unlucky to come up against a new and formidable Labor Opposition Leader in <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a>. Also, he was subjected to media speculation about his drinking habits and his involvements with women. He generated great resentment within his party, and his opponents became increasingly critical of his reliance on an inner circle of advisers – most notably his private secretary <a href="/wiki/Ainsley_Gotto" title="Ainsley Gotto">Ainsley Gotto</a>.
</p><p>The Coalition suffered a 7% swing against it at the <a href="/wiki/1969_Australian_federal_election" title="1969 Australian federal election">1969 election</a>, and Labor outpolled it on the <a href="/wiki/Two-party-preferred_vote" title="Two-party-preferred vote">two-party-preferred vote</a>. During the close election Gorton promised to waive all future government rent on residential leaseholders in Canberra.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> After surviving the election Gorton came through on his promise, giving away an estimated $100&#160;million in equity to leaseholders and abandoning future government rent revenue.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Still, Gorton saw the sizeable 45-seat majority he had inherited from Holt cut down to only seven.  Indeed, the Coalition might have lost government had it not been for the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Labor_Party_(historical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic Labor Party (historical)">Democratic Labor Party</a>'s longstanding practice of preferencing against the ALP.  The Coalition was only assured of a ninth term in government when DLP preferences tipped four marginal seats in Melbourne —the DLP's heartland—to the Liberals.  Had those preferences gone the other way, Whitlam would have become Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_term">Second term</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Second term">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Leadership_challenges_and_resignation">Leadership challenges and resignation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Leadership challenges and resignation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gorton_and_McMahon.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Gorton_and_McMahon.jpg/170px-Gorton_and_McMahon.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="171" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Gorton_and_McMahon.jpg/255px-Gorton_and_McMahon.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Gorton_and_McMahon.jpg/340px-Gorton_and_McMahon.jpg 2x" data-file-width="422" data-file-height="425" /></a><figcaption>Gorton with <a href="/wiki/William_McMahon" title="William McMahon">William McMahon</a> shortly after the unsuccessful leadership challenge in 1969</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the 1969 election, Gorton <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill,_1969" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill, 1969">was unsuccessfully challenged for the Liberal leadership</a> by McMahon and National Development Minister <a href="/wiki/David_Fairbairn_(politician)" title="David Fairbairn (politician)">David Fairbairn</a>; although on this occasion McEwen decided to lift his veto on McMahon. In the subsequent <a href="/wiki/Second_Gorton_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Gorton Ministry">ministerial reshuffle</a>, Gorton reinstated <a href="/wiki/Don_Chipp" title="Don Chipp">Don Chipp</a> and promoted <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Peacock" title="Andrew Peacock">Andrew Peacock</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jim_Killen" class="mw-redirect" title="Jim Killen">Jim Killen</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tom_Hughes_(Australian_politician)" title="Tom Hughes (Australian politician)">Tom Hughes</a> to the ministry, among others. The Coalition suffered a significant primary vote swing in the <a href="/wiki/1970_Australian_Senate_election" title="1970 Australian Senate election">1970 Senate-only election</a>, though seat losses were stemmed to just two and Labor was also judged to have performed poorly.
