</p><p>On 8 March 1971, a challenge was launched when Defence Minister <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a> resigned from the ministry. Fraser had strongly supported Gorton for the leadership three years earlier, but now attacked Gorton on the floor of parliament in his resignation speech, accusing him of disloyalty in a dispute Fraser had with General <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Daly_(general)" title="Thomas Daly (general)">Sir Thomas Daly</a> and of "interference in (his) ministerial responsibilities". Fraser concluded the speech by condemning Gorton as "not fit to hold the great office of Prime Minister".<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Gorton's response on the floor of the House was infamously interrupted when journalist <a href="/wiki/Alan_Ramsey" title="Alan Ramsey">Alan Ramsey</a> shouted "You liar!" to Gorton from the <a href="/wiki/Press_gallery" title="Press gallery">press gallery</a>; realising his error, Ramsey quickly conveyed his gravest apology both to the House and, most particularly, to the Prime Minister. Gorton graciously accepted the apology, while inviting the Opposition to withdraw its motion that Ramsey be immediately arrested by the <a href="/wiki/Serjeant-at-arms" title="Serjeant-at-arms">serjeant-at-arms</a> of the House.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gorton called <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill,_1971" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill, 1971">a Liberal caucus meeting</a> for 10 March 1971 to settle the matter. A motion of confidence in his leadership was tied. Under Liberal caucus rules of the time, a tied vote meant the motion was passed and hence Gorton could have remained as party leader and Prime Minister without further ado.  However, he took it upon himself to resign, saying "Well, that is not a vote of confidence, so the party will have to elect a new leader."<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> A ballot was held and McMahon was elected leader and thus Prime Minister. Australian television marked the end of Gorton's stormy premiership with a newsreel montage accompanied by <a href="/wiki/Frank_Sinatra" title="Frank Sinatra">Sinatra's</a> anthem "<a href="/wiki/My_Way_(song)" class="mw-redirect" title="My Way (song)">My Way</a>".
</p><p>In a surprise move, Gorton contested and won the position of Deputy Leader, forcing McMahon to make him Defence Minister. This situation lasted until August, when Gorton published two articles detailing the problems he had with ministers leaking information from cabinet. McMahon forced Gorton's resignation on the grounds of disloyalty, and Gorton went to the backbench.<sup id="cite_ref-1971archives_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1971archives-83">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years_in_parliament">Final years in parliament</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Final years in parliament">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Nixon_meeting_with_the_Hon.Jack_G_Gorton_-_NARA_-_194715.tif" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/President_Nixon_meeting_with_the_Hon.Jack_G_Gorton_-_NARA_-_194715.tif/lossy-page1-220px-President_Nixon_meeting_with_the_Hon.Jack_G_Gorton_-_NARA_-_194715.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="151" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/President_Nixon_meeting_with_the_Hon.Jack_G_Gorton_-_NARA_-_194715.tif/lossy-page1-330px-President_Nixon_meeting_with_the_Hon.Jack_G_Gorton_-_NARA_-_194715.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/President_Nixon_meeting_with_the_Hon.Jack_G_Gorton_-_NARA_-_194715.tif/lossy-page1-440px-President_Nixon_meeting_with_the_Hon.Jack_G_Gorton_-_NARA_-_194715.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="2057" /></a><figcaption>Gorton meeting with U.S. President <a href="/wiki/Richard_Nixon" title="Richard Nixon">Richard Nixon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Haig" title="Alexander Haig">Alexander Haig</a> in April 1971</figcaption></figure>
<p>A number of polls during McMahon's prime ministership had Gorton as both the preferred Liberal leader and the preferred prime minister. In 1972, businessman <a href="/wiki/David_Hains" title="David Hains">David Hains</a> commissioned a series of polls in marginal electorates that showed the Coalition would significantly increase its vote if Gorton mounted a successful comeback; for instance, polling in the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Henty" title="Division of Henty">Division of Henty</a> found that his return would add eight points to the Liberal vote. However, Gorton generally downplayed the polling and did not mount an active campaign to oust McMahon.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Labor went on to win a nine-seat majority at the <a href="/wiki/1972_Australian_federal_election" title="1972 Australian federal election">1972 election</a>, ending 23 consecutive years of Coalition rule.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> A number of Gorton's contemporaries – including Country Party leader <a href="/wiki/Doug_Anthony" title="Doug Anthony">Doug Anthony</a> and Labor ministers <a href="/wiki/Clyde_Cameron" title="Clyde Cameron">Clyde Cameron</a>, <a href="/wiki/Doug_McClelland" title="Doug McClelland">Doug McClelland</a>, and <a href="/wiki/John_Wheeldon" title="John Wheeldon">John Wheeldon</a> – retrospectively expressed doubts as to whether Whitlam could have won if Gorton had returned to the prime ministership. <a href="/wiki/Rupert_Murdoch" title="Rupert Murdoch">Rupert Murdoch</a>, whose newspapers endorsed the Labor Party, stated in 2000 that "we would most certainly have supported the re-election of a Gorton government in 1972. And he would have won!".<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The seat of Henty, which became a particular point of Hains' polling as Hains made his case for Gorton to be restored as prime minister, was narrowly retained by the Liberals despite a huge swing towards Labor.
</p><p>McMahon resigned as Liberal leader a few weeks after the 1972 election. Gorton was one of five candidates who stood <a href="/wiki/1972_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="1972 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">the resulting leadership ballot</a>, but polled only the fourth-highest total as <a href="/wiki/Billy_Snedden" title="Billy Snedden">Billy Snedden</a> won a narrow victory over <a href="/wiki/Nigel_Bowen" title="Nigel Bowen">Nigel Bowen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Snedden subsequently appointed him to the opposition frontbench as spokesman for urban and regional development, the environment, and conservation.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Soon after being sworn in as prime minister, Whitlam responded to a congratulatory letter from Gorton by promising to "advance some of the causes which you were the first Australian Prime Minister to identify". Over the following years, several failed initiatives from Gorton's prime ministership were passed into law by the <a href="/wiki/Whitlam_government" title="Whitlam government">Whitlam government</a>. This included the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Film,_Television_and_Radio_School" title="Australian Film, Television and Radio School">Australian Film, Television and Radio School</a> and the assertion of sovereignty over the territorial seabed and continental shelf.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_liberalism">Social liberalism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Social liberalism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Early in 1973, Gorton stated his public support of "<a href="/wiki/Abortion" title="Abortion">abortion</a> on request, under certain conditions"; he was opposed to "compulsory pregnancy". He nonetheless voted against <a href="/wiki/David_McKenzie_(Victorian_politician)" title="David McKenzie (Victorian politician)">David McKenzie</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tony_Lamb" title="Tony Lamb">Tony Lamb</a>'s private member's bill to legalise abortion in the Australian Capital Territory, as he believed it did not provide clear enough guidelines for medical practitioners. He preferred the conditions of the <a href="/wiki/Menhennitt_ruling" class="mw-redirect" title="Menhennitt ruling">Menhennitt ruling</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Gorton was also a supporter of no-fault divorce. During the debate over what became the <i><a href="/wiki/Family_Law_Act_1975" title="Family Law Act 1975">Family Law Act 1975</a></i>, he <a href="/wiki/Cross_the_floor" class="mw-redirect" title="Cross the floor">crossed the floor</a> to oppose a Coalition amendment which he thought complicated the requirements for divorce through separation.<sup id="cite_ref-p377_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p377-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 1973, Gorton introduced a motion in the House of Representatives calling for the <a href="/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Australia" title="LGBT rights in Australia">decriminalisation of homosexuality</a>, co-sponsored by Labor's <a href="/wiki/Moss_Cass" title="Moss Cass">Moss Cass</a>. It was modelled on the recommendations of the UK's <a href="/wiki/Wolfenden_report" title="Wolfenden report">Wolfenden report</a>. While noting his personal objections to homosexuality, Gorton stated that most gay people "hurt no one, harm no one and yet have this hanging over them". He dismissed arguments that decriminalisation would violate "God's law", noting that many religious leaders were in favour of a change, and stated that the existing law had led to "<a href="/wiki/Gay_bashing" title="Gay bashing">bashing</a>", blackmail, and suicides. The motion passed by 24 votes, with all parties receiving a <a href="/wiki/Conscience_vote" title="Conscience vote">conscience vote</a>. However, it was of no legal effect as homosexuality law was the province of state laws and territory ordinances.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:1_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Resignation_from_the_Liberal_Party">Resignation from the Liberal Party</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Resignation from the Liberal Party">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Whitlam was re-elected at the <a href="/wiki/1974_Australian_federal_election" title="1974 Australian federal election">1974 election</a>. Gorton was dropped from shadow cabinet after the election, as was McMahon.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> In November 1974, following an unsuccessful attempt to install <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a> as Liberal leader, he condemned those involved and stated they had caused "irreparable damage" to the party.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> On 3 March 1975, as leadership tensions continued to build, Gorton announced that he would not recontest his seat in parliament at the next election, citing his unwillingness to be a perpetual backbencher. He also stated: "If Fraser got in, it would be a disaster. He is extreme right-wing. The Liberal Party can't be a right-leaning affair."<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> When Fraser won <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill,_1975" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill, 1975">a leadership spill</a> on 21 March, Gorton reportedly stormed out of the meeting and slammed the door behind him.<sup id="cite_ref-p377_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p377-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 23 May 1975, Gorton announced his resignation from the Liberal Party and his intention to be an independent candidate for one of the new <a href="/wiki/Australian_Capital_Territory" title="Australian Capital Territory">Australian Capital Territory</a> (ACT) Senate seats. He hoped to join <a href="/wiki/Steele_Hall_(Australian_politician)" class="mw-redirect" title="Steele Hall (Australian politician)">Steele Hall</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Crossbench" class="mw-redirect" title="Crossbench">crossbench</a> and secure the <a href="/wiki/Balance_of_power_(parliament)" title="Balance of power (parliament)">balance of power</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-p377_91-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p377-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Opinion polling shortly after his announcement showed him winning 55 percent of the primary vote. His candidacy received endorsements from the small local branches of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Movement_(Australia)" title="Liberal Movement (Australia)">Liberal Movement</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Australia_Party" title="Australia Party">Australia Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> Gough Whitlam was dismissed as prime minister on 11 November, sparking <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_federal_election" title="1975 Australian federal election">an early election</a> on 13 December. During <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_constitutional_crisis" title="1975 Australian constitutional crisis">the preceding constitutional crisis</a>, Gorton had denounced Fraser's actions in blocking <a href="/wiki/Supply_(political_science)" class="mw-redirect" title="Supply (political science)">supply</a> in the Senate. Gorton advocated a vote and "a resounding victory" for Labor in election ads aired nationally, in protest against the dismissal of the Whitlam government.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> In the election, he polled 11.9 percent of the ACT Senate vote running on a two-man ticket with <a href="/wiki/Harold_Hird" title="Harold Hird">Harold Hird</a>. It was the second-highest percentage for an independent candidate nation-wide, after <a href="/wiki/Brian_Harradine" title="Brian Harradine">Brian Harradine</a>'s 12.8 percent in Tasmania, but nowhere near enough to win election. He had campaigned mainly on local issues, which obscured his candidacy somewhat in an election that was a virtual referendum on the Whitlam government.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement_and_death">Retirement and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Retirement and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Grave_of_John_Gorton,_Melbourne_General_Cemetery.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Grave_of_John_Gorton%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery.jpg/220px-Grave_of_John_Gorton%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Grave_of_John_Gorton%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery.jpg/330px-Grave_of_John_Gorton%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Grave_of_John_Gorton%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery.jpg/440px-Grave_of_John_Gorton%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4032" data-file-height="3024" /></a><figcaption>Grave of Sir John within the 'Prime Ministers Garden' at <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_General_Cemetery" title="Melbourne General Cemetery">Melbourne General Cemetery</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Gorton retired to <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a>, where he largely kept out of the political limelight. However, in March 1983, he congratulated <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a> "for rolling that bastard Fraser" at <a href="/wiki/1983_Australian_federal_election" title="1983 Australian federal election">that year's election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1977, Gorton was recruited to record a series of three-minute radio broadcasts on current affairs, titled "Sir John Gorton's viewpoints". He wrote and recorded around 400 segments over the following four years, which were syndicated and broadcast by over 80 radio stations around the country. His broadcasts covered a wide range of issues – he supported the decriminalisation of <a href="/wiki/Marijuana" class="mw-redirect" title="Marijuana">marijuana</a> and <a href="/wiki/Prostitution" title="Prostitution">prostitution</a>, called on Australians to welcome <a href="/wiki/Vietnamese_boat_people" title="Vietnamese boat people">Vietnamese boat people</a>, opposed the creation of <a href="/wiki/Special_Broadcasting_Service" title="Special Broadcasting Service">SBS</a>, denounced "the cacophony known as modern music", opposed Aboriginal land rights, supported <a href="/wiki/Uranium" title="Uranium">uranium</a> mining, and opposed <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_Australia" title="Republicanism in Australia">republicanism</a>. He frequently criticised the <a href="/wiki/Fraser_government" title="Fraser government">Fraser government</a>, but grudgingly admired Fraser's ability to get his way as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 1990s, Gorton quietly rejoined the Liberal Party; <a href="/wiki/John_Hewson" title="John Hewson">John Hewson</a> credited himself with "returning Gorton to the fold."<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> In 1993, Gorton was invited to open the Liberal Party's campaign headquarters for the <a href="/wiki/1993_Australian_federal_election" title="1993 Australian federal election">1993 election</a>. He endorsed Hewson's <a href="/wiki/Fightback!_(policy)" title="Fightback! (policy)">Fightback!</a> package.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> In his old age he was rehabilitated by the Liberals; his 90th birthday party was attended by Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a> who said at the event: "He (Gorton) was a person who above everything else was first, second and last an Australian." Although he was back within Liberal circles, he never forgave Fraser; as late as 2002 he told his biographer Ian Hancock that he still could not tolerate being in the same room as Fraser.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gorton died at Sydney's <a href="/wiki/St_Vincent%27s_Hospital,_Sydney" title="St Vincent&#39;s Hospital, Sydney">St Vincent's Hospital</a> at the age of 90 in May 2002.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> A <a href="/wiki/State_funeral" title="State funeral">state funeral</a><sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> and memorial service was held on 30 May at <a href="/wiki/St_Andrew%27s_Cathedral,_Sydney" title="St Andrew&#39;s Cathedral, Sydney">St Andrews' Cathedral</a> where extremely critical remarks of Fraser, who was in attendance with wife <a href="/wiki/Tamie_Fraser" title="Tamie Fraser">Tamie</a>, were delivered during the eulogy by Gorton's former Attorney-General <a href="/wiki/Tom_Hughes_(Australian_politician)" title="Tom Hughes (Australian politician)">Tom Hughes</a>. Current and former Prime Ministers Howard, <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a> were also in attendance.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Gorton was cremated after a private service and his ashes interred within the 'Prime Ministers Garden' at <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_General_Cemetery" title="Melbourne General Cemetery">Melbourne General Cemetery</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriages_and_family">Marriages and family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Marriages and family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_and_Bettina_Gorton_1970.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/John_and_Bettina_Gorton_1970.jpg/170px-John_and_Bettina_Gorton_1970.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="162" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/John_and_Bettina_Gorton_1970.jpg/255px-John_and_Bettina_Gorton_1970.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/John_and_Bettina_Gorton_1970.jpg/340px-John_and_Bettina_Gorton_1970.jpg 2x" data-file-width="697" data-file-height="665" /></a><figcaption>John and <a href="/wiki/Bettina_Gorton" title="Bettina Gorton">Bettina Gorton</a> at the opening of Parliament on 3 March 1970</figcaption></figure>
<p>During a holiday in Spain while an undergraduate, Gorton met <a href="/wiki/Bettina_Gorton" title="Bettina Gorton">Bettina Brown</a> of <a href="/wiki/Bangor,_Maine" title="Bangor, Maine">Bangor, Maine</a>, United States. She was a language student at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Paris" title="University of Paris">Sorbonne</a>. This meeting came about through Gorton's friend from Oxford, Arthur Brown, who was Bettina's brother. In 1935, Gorton and Brown were married in Oxford. After his studies were finished, they settled in Australia, taking over his father's orchard, "Mystic Park", at <a href="/w/index.php?title=Lake_Kangaroo,_Victoria&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Lake Kangaroo, Victoria (page does not exist)">Lake Kangaroo</a> near <a href="/wiki/Kerang" title="Kerang">Kerang</a>, Victoria. They had three children: Joanna, Michael, and Robin. Gorton's first wife died of cancer in 1983. In 1993, he remarried to Nancy Home (née Elliott), a long-time acquaintance.<sup id="cite_ref-naabg_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naabg-107">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gorton's granddaughter is poet and novelist <a href="/wiki/Lisa_Gorton" title="Lisa Gorton">Lisa Gorton</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religious_beliefs">Religious beliefs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Religious beliefs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Gorton was a nominal Christian at least in the early part of his life, but was not a churchgoer. Some sources have identified him as agnostic or even atheist. He attended Anglican schools, and was influenced by the <a href="/wiki/Christian_socialist" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian socialist">Christian socialist</a> views of <a href="/wiki/James_Ralph_Darling" title="James Ralph Darling">James Ralph Darling</a>, his headmaster at <a href="/wiki/Geelong_Grammar" class="mw-redirect" title="Geelong Grammar">Geelong Grammar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> In a 1948 speech, Gorton said that "the story of Christianity is the most tremendous in the history of the world".<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> However, in the lead-up to the <a href="/wiki/1999_Australian_republic_referendum" title="1999 Australian republic referendum">1999 referendum</a> he publicly opposed mentioning God in the preamble. According to his biographer Ian Hancock: "Gorton may not have been a believing, let alone a practising, Christian, and when he spoke of religion of 'the soul' he did not have a particular faith in mind. Rather, his religion was founded upon the injunction in the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Deuteronomy" title="Book of Deuteronomy">Book of Deuteronomy</a>: 'Man doth not live by bread only'."<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Honours">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Gorton_bust.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/John_Gorton_bust.jpg/170px-John_Gorton_bust.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/John_Gorton_bust.jpg/255px-John_Gorton_bust.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/John_Gorton_bust.jpg/340px-John_Gorton_bust.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="1600" /></a><figcaption>Bust of John Gorton by sculptor <a href="/wiki/Victor_Greenhalgh" title="Victor Greenhalgh">Victor Greenhalgh</a> located in the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Ministers_Avenue" title="Prime Ministers Avenue">Prime Ministers Avenue</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Ballarat_Botanical_Gardens" title="Ballarat Botanical Gardens">Ballarat Botanical Gardens</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Gorton was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">Privy Counsellor</a> in 1968, a <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Companions_of_Honour" title="Order of the Companions of Honour">Companion of Honour</a> in 1971,<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> a <a href="/wiki/Knight_Grand_Cross_of_the_Order_of_St_Michael_and_St_George" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight Grand Cross of the Order of St Michael and St George">Knight Grand Cross of the Order of St Michael and St George</a> in 1977,<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> and a <a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of Australia">Companion of the Order of Australia</a> in 1988.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> He was awarded the <a href="/wiki/Centenary_Medal" title="Centenary Medal">Centenary Medal</a> in 2001.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Railways_CL_class" title="Commonwealth Railways CL class">Commonwealth Railways CL class</a> locomotive CL1 was named <i>John Gorton</i> in February 1970.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gorton&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<p><b><a href="/wiki/Gorton_government" title="Gorton government">Prime Minister of Australia</a></b>
</p>
<hr />
<p><b><span class="nowrap">Term of government (1968-1971)</span></b>
</p>

<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-54"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-54">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In Australia, ministers are drawn from both the House of Representatives and the Senate. To allow the Opposition to ask questions about all governmental actions in both houses, some ministers assume responsibility for portfolios held by ministers from the other chamber.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-56"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-56">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">His formal title was "Minister in charge of Commonwealth Activities in Education and Research under the Prime Minister".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002103_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002103-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-58"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-58">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">There was no separate education department at the time, with the portfolio instead handled by the Commonwealth Education Office within the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister%27s_Department_(Australia)" title="Prime Minister&#39;s Department (Australia)">Prime Minister's Department</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002104_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002104-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-63"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-63">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Between 1963 and 1966, the number of Commonwealth scholarships on offer rose from 4,701 to 8,069, an increase of 72 percent, while the number of new university entrants rose from 17,311 to 24,196, an increase of 40 percent. Total federal funding of education rose by 77 percent from the 1964–65 budget to the 1967–68 budget.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHancock2002121–122_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHancock2002121–122-62">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
